<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Energy, exceptional sets, and the final estimate

The construction has supplied a bounded comparison function on each usable cell. Its lattice gradients are controlled by the total traversal energy, and a hole fails to match that function only near a short-time flag or in a small closed class. We now sum these three contributions and choose the auxiliary scales.

<a id="stmt-lem-cell-resolvent"></a>
<div id="lem:cell-resolvent" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cell-resolvent"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Cellwise approximation of the resolvent). *There are random numbers $c_B\in[-1,1]$, one per cell, such that $$\label{eq:cell-resolvent}
 \mathbb E\sum_B\sum_{\substack{i\in\mathcal H\\x_i\in B}}
        (u_i-c_B)^2\le C_\beta N R^{-1/20}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cell-resolvent"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cell-resolvent</summary>

*Proof.* On a usable cell let $c_B=|B|^{-1}\sum_{x\in B}U_B(x)$; on an unusable cell put $c_B=0$. The rectangular lattice Poincaré inequality, whose constant is at most $CR^2$ for the stated side lengths, gives $$\sum_{x\in B}|U_B(x)-c_B|^2
 \le CR^2\sum_{\substack{\{x,x'\}\subset B\\|x-x'|_1=1}}
                  |U_B(x)-U_B(x')|^2.$$ It holds in the chosen lift, also for a cell covering an entire coordinate circle: only the internal rectangle edges are needed. Use Lemma <a href="#lem:hull-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hull-comparison">18</a> both here and for the nonexceptional differences $u_i-U_B(x_i)$. There are at most $\ell$ holes above a site. In summing all the local energy sums, any fixed torus $e_i$ occurs at most $C_\beta n^3$ times, since the original fine sites, over all cells, partition the torus. Periodic copies used to construct the windows do not add summation sites. Equation <a href="#eq:traversal-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:traversal-energy">[eq:traversal-energy]</a> therefore bounds these contributions by $$C_\beta R^2n^9\delta^{-1}qN.$$

<a id="eq:comparison-errors"></a>
All remaining squared differences are at most four. A fixed fine site has at most $Cn^3$ boxes in its short-flag neighborhood; its exceptional probability is at most $C_\beta n^3\delta$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:bad-box-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bad-box-tail">16</a>. The total number of holes in $\Gamma$-components of size at most $C_\beta n^3$ is at most $C_\beta n^3$ times the number of components, whose expectation is bounded by <a href="#eq:component-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:component-count">[eq:component-count]</a>. Finally sum <a href="#eq:unusable-cell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unusable-cell">[eq:unusable-cell]</a> with weights at most $\ell|B|$. Together these estimates give $$\label{eq:comparison-errors}
 \frac1N\mathbb E\sum_B\sum_{\substack{i\in\mathcal H\\x_i\in B}}
      (u_i-c_B)^2
 \le C_\beta\left(
 R^2n^9\delta^{-1}q+n^3\delta+n^3q
                   +R^3e^{-cn^{\lambda_1}}\right).$$

Take now $$\delta=R^{-1/8},\qquad n=\lceil R^{1/200}\rceil.$$ At fixed $\beta$, sufficiently large $R$ meets all preceding smallness conditions, including $10^6dn\le R\le L$, and the short-flag threshold in Lemma <a href="#lem:bad-box-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bad-box-tail">16</a>. The first three terms on the right of <a href="#eq:comparison-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-errors">[eq:comparison-errors]</a> are, respectively, $$O(R^{-33/100}),\qquad O(R^{-11/100}),\qquad
 O(R^{-497/200}).$$ The fourth is smaller than any negative power of $R$. This proves <a href="#eq:cell-resolvent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cell-resolvent">[eq:cell-resolvent]</a> with the stated, nonoptimal exponent. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cell-resolvent-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cell-resolvent</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:weak-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:weak-field">12</a>.* The partition has at most $CV/R^3$ cells and each has volume comparable to $R^3$. Lemmas <a href="#lem:cell-resolvent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cell-resolvent">19</a> and <a href="#lem:hole-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hole-cancellation">14</a>, together with $|c_B|\le1$, give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\bigl|\langle f_{\mathcal H},u\rangle\bigr|
 &\le \mathbb E\sum_B\sum_{i\in\mathcal H,\,x_i\in B}|u_i-c_B|
       +\sum_B\mathbb E\left|\sum_{i\in\mathcal H,\,x_i\in B}f_i\right|\\
 &\le C_\beta N R^{-1/40}
          +CV(qR^3)^{-1/2}.
\end{aligned}$$ In <a href="#eq:transverse-resolvent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transverse-resolvent">[eq:transverse-resolvent]</a>, add the diagonal terms inside the resolvent sum and discard their nonnegative subtraction. Since $u=qD^{-1}f_{\mathcal H}$, this yields $$\frac qV\langle D_f^2\rangle_b
 \le \frac{qN}{4V}+
       \frac1{2V}\mathbb E\bigl|\langle f_{\mathcal H},u\rangle\bigr|
 \le C_\beta\bigl(q+R^{-1/40}+R^{-1/4}\bigr).$$ This is <a href="#eq:weak-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weak-field">[eq:weak-field]</a>. The assumptions needed for the sparse-pin estimate hold because $q=R^{-5/2}\ge L^{-5/2}$ and the lower bound on $R$ can include $L_*(\beta)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:zero-field"></a>
### Removing the field and comparing spatial scales

Proposition <a href="#prop:weak-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:weak-field">12</a> controls an ordinary transverse square in a small positive field. We first turn this estimate into an exponential bound at zero field. The bound is weak for one prescribed test, but it holds uniformly over enough tests to compare magnetization averages in a cell with those in its children. Throughout this section, $\beta$ is sufficiently large and fixed.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Curvature of a logarithmic Laplace transform

The analytic step uses the following strip lemma. Its positivity is essential: small curvature at the origin will then control curvature on a real interval whose length grows logarithmically with the spatial scale.

<a id="stmt-lem-strip-curvature"></a>
<div id="lem:strip-curvature" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:strip-convolution"></a>
<a id="eq:strip-curvature-growth"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Strip curvature). *Let $F$ be holomorphic on $\{z\in\mathbb C:|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|<a_0\}$, real and even on the real axis, with $F(0)=0$. Suppose that, for some $K<\infty$, $$0\le F(x)\le K|x|,
  \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Re}}F(x+iy)\le F(x)
  \quad(x\in\mathbb R,\ |y|<a_0).$$ For each $a\in(0,a_0)$ there is a strictly positive Schwartz function $J_a$ such that, with $g_a(x)=F(x)-\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}F(x+ia)$, $$\label{eq:strip-convolution}
 F''=J_a*g_a,
 \qquad
 J_a=\frac{2}{a^2}(h_a*h_a),
 \qquad
 h_a(x)=\frac{\pi}{2a}\mathop{\mathrm{sech}}^2\!\left(\frac{\pi x}{a}\right).$$ In particular, with $c_a=2\pi/a$, $$\label{eq:strip-curvature-growth}
  0\le F''(x)\le e^{c_a|x|}F''(0),
  \qquad
  F(x)\le \frac{F''(0)}{c_a^2}
       \bigl(e^{c_a|x|}-1-c_a|x|\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-strip-curvature"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-strip-curvature</summary>

*Proof.* Put $u(x,y)=\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}F(x+iy)$. Schwarz reflection makes $u$ even in $y$. We first justify Fourier transformation in the unbounded horizontal direction. Fix $a'<a_0$. Around each vertical segment $\{x+iy:|y|\le a'\}$ choose a rectangle of fixed width and height strictly inside the strip. On that rectangle the harmonic function $M_x-u$, with $M_x=C(1+|x|)$ and $C$ sufficiently large, is positive. Its value at $(x,0)$ is at most $C(1+|x|)$ because $F(x)\ge0$. Harnack’s inequality, followed by interior derivative estimates, gives $$|u(x,y)|+|\partial_x^j\partial_y^k u(x,y)|
      \le C_{a',j,k}(1+|x|),\qquad |y|\le a',$$ where for derivatives one first uses a slightly larger closed substrip. Thus $u(\cdot,y)$ and all derivatives needed below are tempered distributions, smoothly depending on $y$.

Use the Fourier convention $\widehat h(\xi)=\int e^{-ix\xi}h(x)\,\mathrm dx$. The harmonic equation becomes $\partial_y^2\widehat u=\xi^2\widehat u$. This equation is solved *locally in frequency*: multiply by a smooth function of compact support in $\xi$ and solve the resulting distribution-valued ordinary differential equation. Multiplication by its fundamental matrix is then legitimate, since $\xi$ remains in a compact set. The initial data are $\widehat u(\xi,0)=\widehat F(\xi)$ and $\partial_y\widehat u(\xi,0)=0$. Consequently $$\widehat u(\xi,a)=\cosh(a\xi)\widehat F(\xi)$$ on every compact frequency interval. We do not multiply an arbitrary tempered distribution globally by the exponentially growing function $\cosh(a\xi)$.

The smooth function $$m_a(\xi)=\frac{\xi^2}{\cosh(a\xi)-1},
 \qquad m_a(0)=\frac{2}{a^2},$$ is Schwartz. The preceding local identity gives $m_a\widehat g_a=-\xi^2\widehat F$ on every compact frequency interval, and hence as tempered distributions. To identify its inverse Fourier transform, the substitution $r=e^{2\pi x/a}$ and the beta integral give $$\widehat h_a(\xi)
 =\int_0^\infty\frac{r^{-ia\xi/(2\pi)}}{(1+r)^2}\,\mathrm dr
 =\frac{a\xi/2}{\sinh(a\xi/2)}.$$ The value at zero is understood by continuity. It follows that $m_a=(2/a^2)\widehat h_a^{\,2}$, proving <a href="#eq:strip-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strip-convolution">[eq:strip-convolution]</a>. The convolution is an ordinary convergent integral because $g_a$ has polynomial growth. Its distributional identity therefore holds pointwise.

Both $h_a$ and $J_a$ are strictly positive Schwartz functions. Moreover, $|h_a'|\le c_a h_a$, so differentiation under the convolution gives $|J_a'|\le c_a J_a$. Hence $J_a(x-t)\le e^{c_a|x|}J_a(-t)$. Since $g_a\ge0$, integration proves the first inequality in <a href="#eq:strip-curvature-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strip-curvature-growth">[eq:strip-curvature-growth]</a>. Integrating twice, using $F(0)=F'(0)=0$ and evenness, proves the second. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### A zero-field exponential bound

<a id="stmt-prop-zero-field"></a>
<div id="prop:zero-field" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:zero-exp"></a>
<a id="eq:zero-tail"></a>
**Proposition 21**. *Fix $S$ and a sufficiently large finite $\beta$. There are $R_0=R_0(S,\beta)$, $C_\beta<\infty$, and $\kappa>0$ with the following property. Let $L\ge R\ge R_0$, with $L$ even, and partition $\Lambda_L$ into rectangular cells whose side lengths lie in $[R/2,4R]$. If $f:\Lambda_L\to[-1,1]$ has sum zero on every cell, then, for $q=R^{-5/2}$, $b=-\log(1-q)$, and any spin component $a$, $$\label{eq:zero-exp}
 \log\left\langle
   \exp\left(bx\sum_{z\in\Lambda_L}f_zS_z^a\right)
 \right\rangle
 \le C_\beta bV,
 \qquad x\in\mathbb R,\quad |x|\le\kappa\log R.$$ Consequently there are $A_\beta,c>0$ such that the spectral measurement of $D_f=\sum_z f_zS_z^a$ in the zero-field state satisfies $$\label{eq:zero-tail}
 \mathbb P\left\{|D_f|>\frac{A_\beta V}{\log R}\right\}
       \le 2e^{-cbV}.$$ All bounds are uniform over the indicated partitions and tests.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-zero-field"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-zero-field</summary>

*Proof.* It suffices first to take $a=3$ and put the field in direction $Y$. Write $D_f=\sum_z f_zZ_z$ and define $$F(z)=\frac{1}{bV}\log\langle e^{bzD_f}\rangle_b.$$ Since $[H_0,M_L^2]=0$, cyclicity of the trace gives $$\langle e^{bzD_f}\rangle_b
 =\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\rm phys}
       \bigl(e^{H_0}e^{bM_L^2/2}e^{bzD_f}e^{bM_L^2/2}\bigr)}
       {\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\rm phys}e^{H_0+bM_L^2}}.$$ The middle three factors form the restriction of a tensor product of single-slot sandwich matrices. Lemma <a href="#lem:strip-maps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:strip-maps">5</a>, applied to $e^{bs^2/2}e^{bzf_zs^3}e^{bs^2/2}$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:trace-zero-free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trace-zero-free">4</a> show that the expression inside the logarithm has no zeros in a fixed strip $|\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z|<a_0$. Here $R_0$ is large enough that $b\le b_*$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:strip-maps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:strip-maps">5</a>. Choose the analytic logarithm that is real on the real axis and vanishes at zero.

This is the logarithm of an *ordinary* Laplace transform: the spectral measure of $D_f$ in the tilted Gibbs state is a probability measure, even though $D_f$ need not commute with its density matrix. Rotation by $\pi$ around the field axis makes this measure even. Therefore $F$ is even, $F(x)\ge0$, and, since $\|D_f\|\le SV$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}F(x+iy)\le F(x)\le S|x|.$$ In particular, $$F''(0)=\frac bV\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_b(D_f)
       =\frac bV\langle D_f^2\rangle_b
       \le C_\beta R^{-\eta}$$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:weak-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:weak-field">12</a>, with $\eta=1/40$, and $b/q\le2$ for large $R$. This derivative is an ordinary variance, not the Duhamel covariance obtained by perturbing the Hamiltonian inside its exponential.

Fix $a\in(0,a_0)$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:strip-curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:strip-curvature">20</a>. Choose $\kappa>0$ so that $c_a\kappa<\eta/2$. Equation <a href="#eq:strip-curvature-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strip-curvature-growth">[eq:strip-curvature-growth]</a> then gives, uniformly over our tests, $$0\le F(x)\le C_\beta R^{-\eta/2},
 \qquad |x|\le\kappa\log R.$$ To remove the field, let $\gamma_0$ and $\gamma_b$ be the normalized zero-field and tilted density matrices. Since $H_0$ commutes with $M_L^2$ and $\|M_L^2\|\le SV$, $$\gamma_0\le e^{2bSV}\gamma_b.$$ Taking the trace against the positive operator $e^{bxD_f}$ costs at most $2bSV$ in its logarithm and proves <a href="#eq:zero-exp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-exp">[eq:zero-exp]</a> for $a=3$. Rotation invariance of $\gamma_0$ gives every common axis. Finally, exponential Markov at $x=\pm\kappa\log R$, with $\kappa A_\beta>C_\beta+c$, proves <a href="#eq:zero-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-tail">[eq:zero-tail]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Comparison through nested partitions

For a rectangular cell $C$ and an axis $a$, write $$\overline S_C^a=\frac1{|C|}\sum_{x\in C}S_x^a.$$ All averages in a single fixed axis commute. The next proposition compares their ordinary squares; it makes no joint-measurement assertion about the three spin components.

<a id="stmt-prop-scale-comparison"></a>
<div id="prop:scale-comparison" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:spatial-comparison"></a>
**Proposition 22**. *Fix $S$ and a sufficiently large finite $\beta$. For every sufficiently large fixed $R_*$ and all sufficiently large even $L$, there is a deterministic rectangular partition $\mathcal P_*(L)$ of $\Lambda_L$ whose side lengths lie in $[R_*/2,4R_*^{10/9}]$ and such that, for $a=1,2,3$, $$\label{eq:spatial-comparison}
 \frac1V\sum_{C\in\mathcal P_*(L)}|C|
       \left\langle(\overline S_C^a)^2\right\rangle
 \le
       \left\langle(M_L^a/V)^2\right\rangle
       +\frac{C_\beta}{\log R_*}+o_L(1).$$ Here $o_L(1)\to0$ at fixed $R_*$ and $\beta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-scale-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-scale-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Set $R_0=L$ and $R_{j+1}=R_j^{9/10}$, and stop at the largest $n$ for which $R_n\ge R_*$. Starting with the one-cell partition, construct nested rectangular partitions $\mathcal P_j$ with cell sides in $[R_j/2,4R_j]$. For example, subdivide each coordinate interval of a parent into nearly equal integer intervals of length comparable to $R_{j+1}$. The ratios $R_j/R_{j+1}$ are large once $R_*$ is large, so every such subdivision meets the stated bounds. The final scale satisfies $R_*\le R_n<R_*^{10/9}$.

Fix an axis and perform its simultaneous site-spin measurement. Let $D(C)$ denote the measured value of $\overline S_C^a$. For one refinement, put $R=R_j$, $r=R_{j+1}$, and define $$T_j=\frac1V\sum_{B\in\mathcal P_j}
             \sum_{\substack{C\in\mathcal P_{j+1}\\C\subset B}}
               |C|\,|D(C)-D(B)|.$$ To express $T_j$ through the tests of Proposition <a href="#prop:zero-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:zero-field">21</a>, assign a sign $\sigma_C\in\{-1,1\}$ to every child and put $$\bar\sigma_B=\frac1{|B|}\sum_{C\subset B}|C|\sigma_C,
 \qquad
 f_x=\frac{\sigma_C-\bar\sigma_B}{2}
       \quad(x\in C\subset B).$$ Then $|f_x|\le1$ and $\sum_{x\in B}f_x=0$. Direct expansion gives $$\sum_x f_xS_x^a
   =\frac12\sum_{B,C\subset B}|C|\sigma_C\bigl(D(C)-D(B)\bigr)$$ in this measurement. Maximizing over the signs therefore gives $VT_j/2$. There are at most $\exp(CV/r^3)$ sign choices, because every child has volume at least $(r/2)^3$. Applying <a href="#eq:zero-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-tail">[eq:zero-tail]</a> to each test and taking a union bound yields $$\mathbb P\{T_j>2A_\beta/\log R\}
 \le 2\exp\{CV R^{-27/10}-cbV\}
 \le 2\exp(-c'VR^{-5/2}).$$ The last inequality holds uniformly after increasing $R_*$, since $b\ge R^{-5/2}$ and $R^{-27/10}=o(R^{-5/2})$.

As $|D(C)-D(B)|\le2S$, this also bounds the expected weighted square increment: $$\mathbb E\left[\frac1V\sum_{B,C\subset B}|C|
                 (D(C)-D(B))^2\right]
 \le \frac{C_\beta}{\log R_j}
       +C\exp(-c'VR_j^{-5/2}).$$ The squares telescope exactly, because $D(B)$ is the weighted mean of its children’s averages: $$\sum_{C\in\mathcal P_{j+1}}|C|D(C)^2
 -\sum_{B\in\mathcal P_j}|B|D(B)^2
 =\sum_{B,C\subset B}|C|(D(C)-D(B))^2.$$ Now $\sum_{j<n}(\log R_j)^{-1}\le C/\log R_*$, whereas $VR_j^{-5/2}\ge L^{1/2}$ and $n=O(\log\log L)$. Summing the expected increments proves <a href="#eq:spatial-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spatial-comparison">[eq:spatial-comparison]</a> with $\mathcal P_*(L)=\mathcal P_n$. The deterministic construction works for all three axes. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:equilibrium"></a>
### Equilibrium states and vanishing replica cross terms

The spatial estimate has a second use besides comparing block averages: it removes certain couplings between independent replicas at a pressure tangent. We give the variational framework first, including the precise order of limits needed for this deduction. The underlying lattice is now $\mathbb Z^3$, with the spin-$S$ matrix algebra at each site. A state is a consistent family of density matrices on finite sets, or equivalently a positive normalized functional on the norm closure of the local matrix algebras. Local convergence means convergence on every local observable. Write $\mathcal S_{\rm TI}$ for the compact convex set of translation-invariant states, and $\tau_x$ for translation by $x$.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### The variational principle and its tangents

For a density matrix $\rho$, let $\mathsf S(\rho)=-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\rho\log\rho)$. If $Q_n=\{0,\ldots,n-1\}^3$ and $\omega\in\mathcal S_{\rm TI}$, its mean entropy and unperturbed interaction expectation per site are $$\mathfrak s(\omega)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\mathsf S(\omega_{Q_n})}{|Q_n|},
 \qquad
 e_0(\omega)=\beta\sum_{r=1}^3
                     \omega(\mathbf S_0\cdot\mathbf S_{e_r}).$$ For a self-adjoint local observable $a$, let $A_L=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_L}\tau_x(a)$, interpreted periodically for $L$ larger than its support, and set $$P_a(t)=\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac1V
                   \log\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{H_0+tA_L},\qquad t\in\mathbb R.$$ The limit uses even $L$, as elsewhere. Finite range and boundedness make free and periodic boundary conditions equivalent for this limit: the difference of their exponents has norm $O(L^2)$.

The following standard variational facts are recalled with proofs to specify the state space on which the later tangent is taken; see (Lanford and Robinson 1968a, Theorems 2–3) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[LanfordRobinson1968Entropy](#ref-LanfordRobinson1968Entropy)] and (Lanford and Robinson 1968b, Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[LanfordRobinson1968Spin](#ref-LanfordRobinson1968Spin)].

<a id="stmt-prop-variational-framework"></a>
<div id="prop:variational-framework" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:variational-pressure"></a>
<a id="eq:pressure-tangent"></a>
**Proposition 23**. *The mean entropy exists on $\mathcal S_{\rm TI}$, is upper semicontinuous and affine, and $$\label{eq:variational-pressure}
 P_a(t)=\max_{\omega\in\mathcal S_{\rm TI}}
       \{\mathfrak s(\omega)+e_0(\omega)+t\omega(a)\}.$$ In particular, writing $P_0=\beta p_{S,\beta}(0)$, the set $$\mathcal E_\beta=
 \{\omega\in\mathcal S_{\rm TI}:
            \mathfrak s(\omega)+e_0(\omega)=P_0\}$$ is a nonempty compact face of $\mathcal S_{\rm TI}$. Every local limit of periodic zero-field Gibbs states belongs to $\mathcal E_\beta$, and $$\label{eq:pressure-tangent}
 P_a'(0+)=\max_{\omega\in\mathcal E_\beta}\omega(a).$$ The extreme points of $\mathcal E_\beta$ are precisely its states that are extreme in $\mathcal S_{\rm TI}$; we call these states ergodic.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-variational-framework"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-variational-framework</summary>

*Proof.* Tile a large cube by translates of $Q_n$ and a remainder of vanishing relative volume. Entropy subadditivity and the bound $\mathsf S(\rho_B)\le |B|\log(2S+1)$ show that $$\mathfrak s(\omega)
       =\inf_{n\ge1}\frac{\mathsf S(\omega_{Q_n})}{|Q_n|}.$$ This proves existence and upper semicontinuity, since each finite-volume entropy is continuous. For a mixture $\rho=\lambda\rho_1+(1-\lambda)\rho_2$, the entropy lies between $\lambda\mathsf S(\rho_1)+(1-\lambda)\mathsf S(\rho_2)$ and this quantity plus the binary entropy of $\lambda$. Division by volume proves affinity of $\mathfrak s$.

Apply the finite-dimensional Gibbs variational principle to the restriction of any $\omega\in\mathcal S_{\rm TI}$ to a large cube. Boundary terms are $o(V)$ in norm, so $P_a(t)\ge\mathfrak s(\omega)+e_0(\omega)+t\omega(a)$. Conversely, choose a locally convergent subsequence of periodic Gibbs states for $H_0+tA_L$, with limit $\omega$. The limit is translation invariant, and local interaction expectations converge. Tiling the large cubes by fixed cubes $Q_n$ gives $$\limsup_{L\to\infty}\frac{\mathsf S(\gamma_{L,t})}{V}
 \le \frac{\mathsf S(\omega_{Q_n})}{|Q_n|}
 \quad\hbox{for every }n.$$ Letting $n\to\infty$ and using the exact finite-volume Gibbs identity gives the reverse variational inequality. At $t=0$ this argument also proves the assertion about every periodic Gibbs local limit. Compactness, upper semicontinuity, and affinity show that $\mathcal E_\beta$ is a nonempty compact face; the assertion about its extreme points follows from the definition of a face.

For completeness, a maximizer $\omega_t$ in <a href="#eq:variational-pressure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variational-pressure">[eq:variational-pressure]</a>, with $t>0$, satisfies $$\max_{\omega\in\mathcal E_\beta}\omega(a)
 \le\frac{P_a(t)-P_0}{t}\le\omega_t(a).$$ As $t\downarrow0$, upper semicontinuity shows that every local limit of $\omega_t$ belongs to $\mathcal E_\beta$: its unperturbed variational value tends to $P_0$, since $|\omega_t(a)|\le\|a\|$. Taking a convergent subsequence proves <a href="#eq:pressure-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pressure-tangent">[eq:pressure-tangent]</a>. ◻

</details>

In the field direction $Y$, the exponent-normalized pressure is $\beta p_{S,\beta}(b/\beta)$. Thus <a id="eq:magnetization-max"></a>$$\label{eq:magnetization-max}
 m=\max_{\omega\in\mathcal E_\beta}\omega(Y_0).$$ Rotation invariance gives the same maximum in every unit direction. Consequently the vector $\mathbf m(\omega)=(\omega(X_0),\omega(Y_0),\omega(Z_0))$ has length at most $m$ for every $\omega\in\mathcal E_\beta$. The face maximizing $\omega(Y_0)$ has an extreme point, so an ergodic equilibrium state attains $m$. Section <a href="#sec:pressure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pressure">7</a> will prove that every ergodic equilibrium state has this same magnetization magnitude.

The following mean-ergodic consequence of extremality is standard for asymptotically commuting lattice translations; see (Kastler and Robinson 1966, Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[KastlerRobinson1966](#ref-KastlerRobinson1966)].

<a id="stmt-lem-ergodic-variance"></a>
<div id="lem:ergodic-variance" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Ergodic averages). *If $\omega$ is an extreme translation-invariant state and $A$ is a self-adjoint local observable, then $$\lim_{n\to\infty}
 \omega\left[\left(
      \frac1{|Q_n|}\sum_{x\in Q_n}\tau_x(A)-\omega(A)\mathbf 1
                   \right)^2\right]=0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-ergodic-variance"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-ergodic-variance</summary>

*Proof.* After shifting and scaling, assume $0\le A\le\mathbf 1$, and denote its average by $\bar A_n$. In the Hilbert space of the state, translations are commuting unitaries. The mean ergodic theorem makes $\bar A_n\Omega$ converge to a vector $\eta$, where $\Omega$ represents the state. Hence $\lambda(C)=\lim_n\omega(C\bar A_n)$ exists for every local $C$.

For local $C\ge0$, locality gives $\|[C^{1/2},\bar A_n]\|\to0$. Therefore $\lambda(C)=\lim_n\omega(C^{1/2}\bar A_nC^{1/2})$, and $0\le\lambda(C)\le\omega(C)$. The functional extends continuously, is translation invariant by the boundary-to-volume estimate for the averages, and satisfies $\lambda(\mathbf 1)=\omega(A)$. Extremality of $\omega$ now gives $\lambda=\omega(A)\omega$: otherwise its normalized parts in $\omega=\lambda+(\omega-\lambda)$ would give a nontrivial convex decomposition into translation-invariant states. Testing on the dense set of local vectors identifies $\eta=\omega(A)\Omega$, which is the asserted variance convergence. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Products of zero-sum observables in distinct replicas

Take $j$ copies of the spin system, with superscripts $[r]$ denoting the replica. The unperturbed exponent is $\sum_{r=1}^jH_0^{[r]}$, and its pressure is $jP_0$. Every translation-invariant equilibrium state for this interaction has each marginal in $\mathcal E_\beta$. Indeed, entropy subadditivity between replicas bounds its variational value by the sum of the marginal variational values, each at most $P_0$; equality of the total forces equality for each marginal. Conversely, products of states in $\mathcal E_\beta$ are replicated equilibrium states, because their entropies and interaction expectations add.

<a id="eq:compressed-zero-sum"></a>
For real coefficients $v_i$ on finitely many qubit slots, define $$D_v^a=\sum_i v_i s_i^a,
 \qquad B_v^a=P_*D_v^aP_*$$ as a physical local operator. On a site’s symmetric subspace, $P_*s_{(x,k)}^aP_*=S_x^a/\ell$. Thus $$\label{eq:compressed-zero-sum}
 B_v^a=\sum_x c_x S_x^a,
 \qquad c_x=\frac1\ell\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}v_{(x,k)},
 \qquad \sum_xc_x=0\quad\hbox{if }\sum_iv_i=0.$$ The next lemma concerns products of these *compressed* operators in different replicas. It does not identify $P_*(D_v^a)^2P_*$ with $(B_v^a)^2$.

<a id="stmt-lem-replica-cross"></a>
<div id="lem:replica-cross" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Replica cross terms). *Fix $S$ and a sufficiently large finite $\beta$. Let $\omega$ be any translation-invariant equilibrium state of $j$ unperturbed replicas. For distinct replicas $r,s$, axes $a,b$, and finitely supported real slot coefficients $v,w$ with $\sum_i v_i=\sum_i w_i=0$, $$\omega\bigl((B_v^a)^{[r]}(B_w^b)^{[s]}\bigr)=0.$$ The same holds for every finite linear combination of translates of such products.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-replica-cross"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-replica-cross</summary>

<a id="eq:replica-relative-entropy"></a>
*Proof.* Write $O=(B_v^a)^{[r]}(B_w^b)^{[s]}$ and choose a fixed cube of side $d$ containing both spatial supports. Let $\rho_L$ be the restriction of $\omega$ to a cube of side $L$, and let $\gamma_L$ be the product of the $j$ periodic zero-field Gibbs density matrices on that cube. With $D(\rho\Vert\gamma)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\rho(\log\rho-\log\gamma)$, $$\label{eq:replica-relative-entropy}
 \frac1V D(\rho_L\Vert\gamma_L)\longrightarrow0.$$ Indeed, expanding $\log\gamma_L$ gives $j$ times the finite-volume pressure minus the entropy density and interaction density of $\rho_L$. Their limits cancel by the equilibrium variational identity; periodic bonds at the boundary change the interaction by $o(V)$.

<a id="eq:replica-exponential"></a>
Fix an integer $R\ge d$ and take $L$ through even multiples of $R$. Partition the cube into cells of side $R$, and let $\mathcal T$ be the translations of the chosen support cube lying wholly within individual cells. In particular, $$\frac{|\mathcal T|}{V}
       =\left(\frac{R-d+1}{R}\right)^3.$$ Write $B_v^a=\sum_xc_xS_x^a$ and $B_w^b=\sum_xd_xS_x^b$, as in <a href="#eq:compressed-zero-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compressed-zero-sum">[eq:compressed-zero-sum]</a>. Choose $C_O\ge\max\{1,S\|c\|_1\|d\|_1\}$ and put $$O'=\frac1{C_O}\sum_{y\in\mathcal T}\tau_y(O).$$ All operators in this sum can be measured simultaneously, using axis $a$ in replica $r$ and axis $b$ in replica $s$. Conditional on any outcome in replica $s$, denote the value of $\tau_y(B_w^b)^{[s]}$ by $b_y$, so $|b_y|\le S\|d\|_1$. As a function of the first replica’s outcomes, $O'$ is then $\sum_z f_zS_z^a$, where $$f_z=\frac1{C_O}\sum_{y\in\mathcal T}c_{z-y}b_y.$$ These coefficients have absolute value at most one. Their sum in each cell vanishes, since each translated support stays in that cell and $\sum_xc_x=0$. Independence of the replicas under $\gamma_L$ and Proposition <a href="#prop:zero-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:zero-field">21</a>, applied conditionally, therefore give $$\label{eq:replica-exponential}
 \log\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\gamma_Le^{\pm\theta O'})\le C_\beta bV,
 \qquad
 b=-\log(1-R^{-5/2}),\quad\theta=\kappa b\log R.$$

For any self-adjoint $K$, the Gibbs variational principle followed by the Golden–Thompson inequality (Golden 1965; Thompson 1965) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Golden1965](#ref-Golden1965), [Thompson1965](#ref-Thompson1965)] gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\rho_LK)
 \le D(\rho_L\Vert\gamma_L)
       +\log\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{\log\gamma_L+K}
 \le D(\rho_L\Vert\gamma_L)+\log\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\gamma_Le^K).$$ Use $K=\theta O'$ or $K=-\theta O'$ according to the sign of $\omega(O)$. Translation invariance and <a href="#eq:replica-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:replica-exponential">[eq:replica-exponential]</a> yield $$\theta\frac{|\mathcal T|}{C_O}|\omega(O)|
 \le D(\rho_L\Vert\gamma_L)+C_\beta bV.$$ First let $L\to\infty$ at this fixed $R$; this order is required because the entropy density in <a href="#eq:replica-relative-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:replica-relative-entropy">[eq:replica-relative-entropy]</a> has no rate. For large $R$, $|\mathcal T|/V$ is bounded below, and we obtain $|\omega(O)|\le C_{\beta,O}/\log R$. Letting $R\to\infty$ proves the claim. Linearity gives the final assertion. ◻

</details>

Thus, for a finite-range replica perturbation that is a sum of single-replica terms and the cross terms in Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-cross" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-cross">25</a>, its pressure tangent depends only on the single-replica terms. The construction in the next section uses this fact to study negative squares of block magnetizations.

<a id="sec:pressure"></a>
### Pressure tangents and the equilibrium magnetization radius

The variational principle bounds the magnetization of every state in $\mathcal E_\beta$ by $m$. We now prove that every ergodic member has magnetization of length exactly $m$. The obstruction to a smaller radius will be analyticity of certain pressure tangents. To obtain that analyticity, we construct random transfer matrices whose traces are nonzero before taking expectations; Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-cross" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-cross">25</a> then identifies the pressure tangents of their moments.

<a id="eq:negative-square-local"></a>
<a id="eq:square-tangent"></a>
For an integer $r\ge2$, write $C_r=\{0,\ldots,r-1\}^3$, and let $\mathcal C_r$ denote all its translates, on the lattice or on a sufficiently large torus. For any such cube $C$, put $X_C=\sum_{x\in C}X_x$, and define $Y_C,Z_C$ in the same way. Fix $B\ge2$ and $W\ge2B$. We will choose a constant $D_B>0$, independent of $W$, and consider the nonpositive local observable $$a_{B,W}=-\frac{Y_{C_B}^2}{B^6}
         -D_B\frac{X_{C_W}^2+Z_{C_W}^2}{W^6}.
 \label{eq:negative-square-local}$$ In an ergodic state with magnetization vector $(0,r,0)$, the transverse term tends to zero as $W\to\infty$ at fixed $B$, while the $Y$ term tends to $-r^2$ as $B\to\infty$, by Lemma <a href="#lem:ergodic-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ergodic-variance">24</a>. The periodic sum of $a_{B,W}$ is denoted by $A_{B,W,L}=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_L}\tau_x(a_{B,W})$. Define $$s_{B,W}(x)=\max_{\omega\in\mathcal E_\beta}
       \{\omega(a_{B,W})+x\omega(Y_0)\},\qquad x\in\mathbb R.
 \label{eq:square-tangent}$$ This function is convex and $S$-Lipschitz. It is even: a rotation by $\pi$ around the $X$-axis preserves $a_{B,W}$ and sends $Y_0$ to $-Y_0$.

<a id="stmt-prop-harmonic-tangent"></a>
<div id="prop:harmonic-tangent" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:harmonic-tangent"></a>
**Proposition 26** (Harmonic pressure tangent). *For every integer $B\ge2$ there is $D_B>0$ such that, for every integer $W\ge2B$, the function in <a href="#eq:square-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:square-tangent">[eq:square-tangent]</a> has a harmonic extension $v_{B,W}$ from the positive real axis to $\mathbb H=\{z\in\mathbb C:\Re z>0\}$ satisfying $$v_{B,W}(x)=s_{B,W}(x)\quad(x>0),\qquad
 v_{B,W}(z)\le s_{B,W}(\Re z)\quad(z\in\mathbb H).
 \label{eq:harmonic-tangent}$$*

</div>

We prove the proposition in the next three subsections. We first express the negative squares in <a href="#eq:negative-square-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-square-local">[eq:negative-square-local]</a> as infinitesimal contributions of real single-slot matrices, together with a small reduction of the ferromagnetic coupling. Shared Gaussian coefficients then turn moments of the random traces into replicated pressures. Finally a second-moment argument selects nonvanishing traces whose logarithms converge to the required harmonic function.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### A Laplacian construction for the negative squares

Recall that there are $\ell=2S$ slots per site and that $P_*$ projects onto the physical spin space. For a real vector $v=(v_i)$ on finitely many slots, write $$D_v^a=\sum_i v_i s_i^a.$$ Our generators will be $iD_v^2$ and $D_v^1,D_v^3$, with $\sum_i v_i=0$. All three are real matrices in the $s^3$ basis, and their exponentials with real coefficients are products of single-slot matrices in $SL_2(\mathbb R)$. Squaring $iD_v^2$ contributes a negative square in the $Y$-direction. The other two generators contribute positive squares; the reduction of the static coupling will cancel their unwanted nearest-neighbor coefficients.

All quadratic coefficients below use *ordered* slot pairs: $$(D_v^a)^2=\sum_{i,j}v_iv_j s_i^a s_j^a.$$ For a cube $C$, let $L_C^{\rm full}$ be the graph Laplacian of the complete graph on its $\ell|C|$ slots. Let $L_C^{\rm bond}$ be the Laplacian with an edge between every pair of slots whose sites are nearest neighbors inside $C$. Thus an off-diagonal Laplacian entry is $-1$ on an edge. These matrices are extended by zero when used on a larger slot set.

The bond graph of $C_B$ is connected. Consequently one can choose $c_B>0$ so that, for every $C\in\mathcal C_B$, <a id="eq:small-cube-covariance"></a>$$Q_C=c_B L_C^{\rm bond}-B^{-6}L_C^{\rm full}\ge0.
 \label{eq:small-cube-covariance}$$ Indeed, both matrices annihilate the constant vector, and on its orthogonal complement the bond Laplacian is positive definite while $L_C^{\rm full}=\ell B^3I$. Decompose $Q_C$ spectrally as $\sum_v vv^{\mathsf T}$. Every vector in this sum is real and has zero sum; use the generators $iD_v^2$ for this list.

<a id="eq:large-cube-covariance"></a>
To cancel the remaining static reduction in the $X,Z$ directions, put $n_{B,W}=(W-B+1)^3$ and, for $C\in\mathcal C_W$, consider $$R_C=D_BW^{-6}L_C^{\rm full}
       -\frac{c_B}{n_{B,W}}
          \sum_{\substack{C'\in\mathcal C_B\\C'\subseteq C}}
                      L_{C'}^{\rm bond}.
 \label{eq:large-cube-covariance}$$ The constant $D_B$ can be chosen so that $R_C\ge0$ for every $W\ge2B$. Here is a uniform bound. Each slot belongs to at most $B^3$ small cubes, and its degree in each bond graph is at most $6\ell$. The sum of the bond Laplacians therefore has norm at most $12\ell B^3$. Since $n_{B,W}\ge W^3/8$, the second term in <a href="#eq:large-cube-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-cube-covariance">[eq:large-cube-covariance]</a> has norm at most $96c_B\ell B^3/W^3$. On the zero-sum subspace, the first term is $D_B\ell/W^3$ times the identity. Thus, for example, <a id="eq:DB-choice"></a>$$D_B=96c_BB^3+1
 \label{eq:DB-choice}$$ suffices. Decompose each $R_C$ as $\sum_v vv^{\mathsf T}$ with real zero-sum vectors, and use both generators $D_v^1$ and $D_v^3$. Choose the lists on one cube of each size and translate them. The resulting families are translation invariant.

Let $T_{B,L}$ be the physical compression of the static reduction <a id="eq:static-reduction"></a>$$-c_B\sum_{C\in\mathcal C_B}
     \sum_{\substack{i,j\text{ slots in }C\\
                     x_i,x_j\text{ nearest neighbors}}}
                   \sum_{a=1}^3s_i^a s_j^a,
 \label{eq:static-reduction}$$ where the inner sum is ordered. Each unordered site bond belongs to $(B-1)B^2$ translates of $C_B$, so $$H_{0,t}:=H_0+tT_{B,L}
   =\bigl(\beta-2tc_B(B-1)B^2\bigr)
                    \sum_{\{x,y\}\text{ bond}}\mathbf S_x\cdot\mathbf S_y.$$ For fixed $B$ this remains ferromagnetic for all sufficiently small $t>0$, uniformly in $L>2W$.

<a id="eq:pulse-identity"></a>
Denote all the generators just constructed by $G_\alpha$. Their quadratic contribution is compressed *after squaring*: $$T_{B,L}+\sum_\alpha P_*G_\alpha^2P_*
       =A_{B,W,L}+C_{B,W}VI
 \label{eq:pulse-identity}$$ on physical space, for a real scalar $C_{B,W}$ independent of $L>2W$. To check the identity, first consider distinct slots $i,j$ in the cube $C$ under consideration. If $E_C(i,j)$ is the indicator of an edge of the bond graph, the coefficients from a small cube in the $Y$-direction are $$\underbrace{-c_BE_C(i,j)}_{\text{static reduction}}
 +\underbrace{\bigl(c_BE_C(i,j)-B^{-6}\bigr)}_{-(Q_C)_{ij}}
 =-B^{-6}.$$ For either the $X$- or the $Z$-direction, the contribution from a large cube is $$(R_C)_{ij}=-D_BW^{-6}
       +\frac{c_B}{n_{B,W}}
          \sum_{\substack{C'\in\mathcal C_B\\C'\subseteq C}}E_{C'}(i,j).$$ Every small cube lies in exactly $n_{B,W}$ large cubes. After summing over translates, the second term cancels the static reduction, leaving the coefficient $-D_BW^{-6}$ on each ordered pair in each large cube. These are precisely the off-diagonal coefficients of $A_{B,W,L}$. The remaining terms have $i=j$ and are scalar because $(s_i^a)^2=I/4$; translation invariance gives the scalar in <a href="#eq:pulse-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pulse-identity">[eq:pulse-identity]</a>. In particular, the identity does not replace $P_*G_\alpha^2P_*$ by $(P_*G_\alpha P_*)^2$, which would in general give different single-replica terms.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### Nonvanishing random traces and replicated moments

<a id="eq:random-partition"></a>
Fix $B,W$ and a sufficiently small $t>0$. For an even $L>2W$ and an integer $k\ge1$, take independent real centered Gaussian variables $\lambda_{r,\alpha}$, $1\le r\le k$, of variance $2t/k$. Fix an order of the generators and define a slot-space layer $$U_r(z)=\left(\prod_\alpha e^{\lambda_{r,\alpha}G_\alpha}\right)
                       e^{(tz/k)\sum_i s_i^2}.$$ Only the complete layer is compressed. With all products taken in their fixed order, define the normalized random entire function $$\mathcal Z_{L,k,t}(z)=
 \frac{e^{-tC_{B,W}V}}{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{H_0}}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\rm phys}\prod_{r=1}^k
       \left[e^{H_{0,t}/k}\bigl(P_*U_r(z)P_*\bigr)\right].
 \label{eq:random-partition}$$ Gaussian exponential moments ensure that all finite products of these functions are integrable, locally uniformly in their arguments.

Every realization of <a href="#eq:random-partition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:random-partition">[eq:random-partition]</a> is nonzero on $\mathbb H$. Although the pulse supports overlap, their fixed ordered product is still a tensor product of single-slot matrices: factors on different slots commute and can be regrouped without changing the order on any one slot. All pulse matrices on a slot lie in $SL_2(\mathbb R)$ and hence act as disk automorphisms in the $s^2$ basis, by Lemma <a href="#lem:strip-maps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:strip-maps">5</a>. On every slot the remaining factor $e^{tzs^2/k}$ sends the closed disk strictly inside the disk when $\Re z>0$. Their composition therefore has the strict mapping property of Lemma <a href="#lem:trace-zero-free" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trace-zero-free">4</a>. To identify the permutation layers, write $E$ for the full-slot exponential of the static interaction with time step $1/k$. It commutes with $P_*$, and cyclicity gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\rm phys}\prod_{r=1}^k
       [e^{H_{0,t}/k}(P_*U_r(z)P_*)]
      =\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\rm slots}\prod_{r=1}^k[(EP_*)U_r(z)].$$ Each $EP_*$ has nonnegative exchange rates and a symmetric seam, so it is a permutation layer of the class allowed in that lemma. The identity uses $P_*^2=P_*$ and does not require $U_r$ to preserve physical space. This proves samplewise nonvanishing, and in particular <a id="eq:sample-harmonic"></a>$$z\longmapsto\frac{1}{tV}\log|\mathcal Z_{L,k,t}(z)|
 \quad\text{is harmonic on }\mathbb H.
 \label{eq:sample-harmonic}$$

<a id="eq:sample-conjugation"></a>
Complex conjugation has a specific sign here. The static matrices, the projections and all pulses are real in the $s^3$ basis, whereas $s^2$ is purely imaginary. Therefore, for the *same* Gaussian sample, $$\overline{\mathcal Z_{L,k,t}(z)}
       =\mathcal Z_{L,k,t}(-\overline z).
 \label{eq:sample-conjugation}$$ The argument on the right may lie outside $\mathbb H$; the function is entire, so this causes no difficulty. In particular its second moment uses the pair $(z,-\overline z)$, not $(z,\overline z)$.

<a id="eq:replicated-moment"></a>
We next compute products of traces using replicas, always sharing the Gaussian variables between replicas. For fixed $j$ and arguments $z_1,\ldots,z_j\in\mathbb C$, the finite-dimensional product formula gives $$\begin{split}
 \lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb E\prod_{r=1}^j\mathcal Z_{L,k,t}(z_r)
  &=\mathcal R^{(j)}_{L,t}(z_1,\ldots,z_j)\\
  &:=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\exp\!\left\{
        \sum_{r=1}^j\bigl(H_0^{[r]}+tA_{B,W,L}^{[r]}
                                      +tz_rM_L^{2,[r]}\bigr)
                         +tA_{{\rm cross},L}^{(j)}\right\}}
                       {(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{H_0})^j}.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:replicated-moment}$$ The limit is locally uniform in the arguments. To verify both the limit and its cross terms, let $P^{(j)}$ be the product of the physical projections in all replicas. For one shared Gaussian variable, $$\mathbb E\exp\!\left(\lambda\sum_{r=1}^jG_\alpha^{[r]}\right)
       =\exp\!\left(\frac tk
                          \left(\sum_{r=1}^jG_\alpha^{[r]}\right)^2\right).$$ Independence allows these expectations to be taken in the prescribed pulse order. After the outer compression, the first-order term is the following operator on physical replica space: <a id="eq:compression-replicas"></a>$$P^{(j)}\left(\sum_{r=1}^jG_\alpha^{[r]}\right)^2P^{(j)}
   =\sum_{r=1}^j(P_*G_\alpha^2P_*)^{[r]}
       +2\sum_{r<s}(P_*G_\alpha P_*)^{[r]}
                         (P_*G_\alpha P_*)^{[s]}.
 \label{eq:compression-replicas}$$ For an imaginary-direction generator $G_\alpha=iD_v^2$, the cross product on the right is $$-(P_*D_v^2P_*)^{[r]}(P_*D_v^2P_*)^{[s]}.$$ For the other two directions it has the positive sign. Thus $$A_{{\rm cross},L}^{(j)}
   =2\sum_\alpha\sum_{r<s}(P_*G_\alpha P_*)^{[r]}
                                      (P_*G_\alpha P_*)^{[s]}$$ is self-adjoint, finite range and translation invariant. Every one of its local summands is a real multiple of a cross term in Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-cross" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-cross">25</a>, since all the vectors $v$ have zero sum.

Using <a href="#eq:pulse-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pulse-identity">[eq:pulse-identity]</a> and distributing the scalar correction in <a href="#eq:random-partition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:random-partition">[eq:random-partition]</a> among the $k$ steps, the expected replicated step is $I+k^{-1}\mathcal H+O(k^{-2})$, where $\mathcal H$ is the exponent in <a href="#eq:replicated-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:replicated-moment">[eq:replicated-moment]</a>. Here $B,W,j,t,L$ are fixed and the error is locally uniform in $(z_1,\ldots,z_j)$. Independence between time steps makes the expected product the $k$th power of this deterministic step. The matrix product limit (Trotter 1959) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Trotter1959](#ref-Trotter1959)] proves <a href="#eq:replicated-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:replicated-moment">[eq:replicated-moment]</a>.

<a id="eq:real-replica-tangent"></a>
For real $x_1,\ldots,x_j$, the numerator defining $\mathcal R^{(j)}_{L,t}$ is positive. The thermodynamic limit followed by the right pressure tangent gives $$\lim_{t\downarrow0}\lim_{L\to\infty}
       \frac{1}{tV}\log\mathcal R^{(j)}_{L,t}(x_1,\ldots,x_j)
           =\sum_{r=1}^j s_{B,W}(x_r).
 \label{eq:real-replica-tangent}$$ Indeed, by <a href="#eq:pressure-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pressure-tangent">[eq:pressure-tangent]</a>, the left side is the maximum of the perturbation density over equilibrium states of the unperturbed replicated interaction. Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-cross" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-cross">25</a> makes the cross density vanish in every such state. Each marginal belongs to $\mathcal E_\beta$, giving the upper bound by the sum on the right. The product of maximizing single-replica states is an equilibrium state and attains that sum. This proves equality.

<a id="eq:complex-trace-bound"></a>
We also need an upper bound at complex arguments. For self-adjoint matrices $A,B$, $$|\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^{A+iB}|\le\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^A.
 \label{eq:complex-trace-bound}$$ For completeness, apply the product formula to $(e^{A/n}e^{iB/n})^n$ and then Schatten Hölder: its trace norm is at most $\|e^{A/n}\|_n^n=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}e^A$, since the intervening factors are unitary. Taking the limit proves the inequality. Applied to <a href="#eq:replicated-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:replicated-moment">[eq:replicated-moment]</a>, it bounds its absolute value by $\mathcal R^{(j)}_{L,t}(\Re z_1,\ldots,\Re z_j)$.

<a id="eq:complex-second-moment"></a>
<a id="eq:product-second-moment"></a>
The limits in <a href="#eq:real-replica-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:real-replica-tangent">[eq:real-replica-tangent]</a> are uniform on compact sets of real arguments in their stated order. Indeed the normalized log traces are $S$-Lipschitz in each real argument, uniformly in $L,t$, by the norm bound $\|M_L^2\|\le SV$; finite nets promote pointwise convergence to uniform convergence. The same bound holds for their limits. Combining this observation with <a href="#eq:sample-conjugation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sample-conjugation">[eq:sample-conjugation]</a>, <a href="#eq:replicated-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:replicated-moment">[eq:replicated-moment]</a> and <a href="#eq:complex-trace-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:complex-trace-bound">[eq:complex-trace-bound]</a>, we obtain the following useful finite parameter statement. Given a compact $K\subset\mathbb H$, finitely many positive real points $x_1,\ldots,x_q$, and $\epsilon>0$, one can choose $t>0$ sufficiently small, then an even $L$ sufficiently large, and finally $k$ sufficiently large, so that, with $T=tV$ and $\mathcal Z=\mathcal Z_{L,k,t}$, <a id="eq:product-first-moment"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E|\mathcal Z(z)|^2
      &\le \exp\{T(2s_{B,W}(\Re z)+\epsilon)\},\qquad z\in K,
          \label{eq:complex-second-moment}\\
 \left|T^{-1}\log\mathbb EU-2\sum_{i=1}^q s_{B,W}(x_i)\right|
      &\le\epsilon,
          \label{eq:product-first-moment}\\
 \left|T^{-1}\log\mathbb EU^2-4\sum_{i=1}^q s_{B,W}(x_i)\right|
      &\le\epsilon,
 \qquad U=\prod_{i=1}^q|\mathcal Z(x_i)|^2.
          \label{eq:product-second-moment}
\end{aligned}$$ The parameter $T$ can be required to exceed any prescribed number, by enlarging $L$ after $t$ has been fixed. For the last two estimates, use the real argument lists $(x_1,-x_1,\ldots,x_q,-x_q)$ and twice that list, together with the evenness of $s_{B,W}$. These products are nonnegative random variables at finite $k$. Their limits at fixed $t,L$ are strictly positive, so the locally uniform product limit also gives the logarithmic estimates. For the first estimate it gives the required upper bound after the finite-volume parameters have been fixed, even when its exponential right side is small.

This order of choices is essential: the matrix product limit is taken at fixed volume; the pressure limit then precedes differentiation at $t=0$. The finite estimates are implemented in the reverse order, choosing $t$, then $L$, then $k$. No zero-free assertion about an averaged partition function has been used.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Selecting harmonic logarithms

We isolate the analytic step to explain how moment information retains the samplewise nonvanishing just proved.

<a id="stmt-lem-harmonic-selection"></a>
<div id="lem:harmonic-selection" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:selection-upper"></a>
<a id="eq:selection-products"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Selection from moment bounds). *Let $s:(0,\infty)\to\mathbb R$ be locally Lipschitz, and enumerate a countable dense subset $D=\{x_1,x_2,\ldots\}$ of $(0,\infty)$ with $x_1=1$. Suppose $F_n$ are random holomorphic functions that are almost surely nonzero on $\mathbb H$, and $T_n\to\infty$. Assume, locally uniformly for $z\in\mathbb H$, that $$\limsup_{n\to\infty}
   \left\{T_n^{-1}\log\mathbb E|F_n(z)|^2-2s(\Re z)\right\}\le0.
 \label{eq:selection-upper}$$ For every $q\ge1$, suppose also that, with $U_n=\prod_{i=1}^q|F_n(x_i)|^2$, $$T_n^{-1}\log\mathbb EU_n\longrightarrow2\sum_i s(x_i),\qquad
 T_n^{-1}\log\mathbb EU_n^2\longrightarrow4\sum_i s(x_i).
 \label{eq:selection-products}$$ Then there is a harmonic function $v$ on $\mathbb H$ with $v(x)=s(x)$ for every $x>0$ and $v(z)\le s(\Re z)$ everywhere.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-harmonic-selection"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-harmonic-selection</summary>

<a id="eq:selection-paley"></a>
*Proof.* Fix $q\ge1$, a compact $K\subset\mathbb H$ containing $x_1,\ldots,x_q$, and $\delta>0$. Put $h_n=T_n^{-1}\log|F_n|$. The Paley–Zygmund inequality (Paley and Zygmund 1932) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[PaleyZygmund1932](#ref-PaleyZygmund1932)] and <a href="#eq:selection-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selection-products">[eq:selection-products]</a> give, for each $\epsilon>0$ and all sufficiently large $n$, $$\mathbb P\{U_n\ge\tfrac12\mathbb EU_n\}
   \ge\frac{(\mathbb EU_n)^2}{4\mathbb EU_n^2}
   \ge\tfrac14 e^{-3\epsilon T_n}.
 \label{eq:selection-paley}$$ We compare this with the probability that $h_n$ exceeds $s(\Re z)+\delta$ somewhere on $K$.

<a id="eq:selection-bad-upper"></a>
Cover $K$ by finitely many disks $D_j$ with concentric doubled disks $\widetilde D_j$ compactly contained in $\mathbb H$, chosen so that the oscillation of $s(\Re z)$ on each $\widetilde D_j$ is at most $\delta/4$. Write $s_j$ for its minimum there. The submean inequality for the nonnegative subharmonic function $|F_n|^2$ gives $$\sup_{D_j}|F_n|^2
    \le C_j\int_{\widetilde D_j}|F_n(z)|^2\,\mathrm dA(z).$$ Using <a href="#eq:selection-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selection-upper">[eq:selection-upper]</a>, the expectation of the right side is at most $C'_j\exp\{T_n(2s_j+\delta/2+\epsilon)\}$ for large $n$. If $h_n(z)>s(\Re z)+\delta$ at any point of $D_j$, then its left side exceeds $\exp\{T_n(2s_j+2\delta)\}$. Markov’s inequality and a sum over the disks therefore yield $$\mathbb P\{h_n(z)>s(\Re z)+\delta\text{ for some }z\in K\}
       \le C_K e^{-(3\delta/2-\epsilon)T_n}.
 \label{eq:selection-bad-upper}$$ Choose $\epsilon>0$ with $4\epsilon<3\delta/2$. Since $T_n\to\infty$, the bound in <a href="#eq:selection-bad-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selection-bad-upper">[eq:selection-bad-upper]</a> is eventually smaller than the lower bound in <a href="#eq:selection-paley" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:selection-paley">[eq:selection-paley]</a>. There is therefore a realization for which both $$h_n(z)\le s(\Re z)+\delta\quad(z\in K),\qquad
 \sum_{i=1}^q h_n(x_i)
     \ge\sum_{i=1}^q s(x_i)-\frac\epsilon2-
                                     \frac{\log2}{2T_n}$$ hold. Subtracting the upper bounds at the other $q-1$ points gives <a id="eq:selection-pointwise"></a>$$s(x_i)-(q-1)\delta-\frac\epsilon2-\frac{\log2}{2T_n}
       \le h_n(x_i)\le s(x_i)+\delta.
 \label{eq:selection-pointwise}$$

Exhaust $\mathbb H$ by compact rectangles, and at stage $q$ enlarge the rectangle to include the first $q$ points. Apply the preceding construction with $\delta=q^{-2}$ and with $\epsilon$ small enough as above, choosing an increasing sequence of indices. The selected harmonic functions are locally bounded above, and their values converge to $s$ at each point of $D$. Their values at $1$ in particular remain bounded below. On any connected relatively compact subdomain containing $1$, subtracting these functions from a common upper bound and using Harnack’s inequality gives local lower bounds as well. Interior estimates and a diagonal subsequence yield a locally uniform harmonic limit $v$. The compact upper bounds give $v(z)\le s(\Re z)$; the values on $D$, followed by continuity, give $v(x)=s(x)$ for every $x>0$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-harmonic-selection-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-harmonic-selection</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:harmonic-tangent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:harmonic-tangent">26</a>.* Fix $B,W$ and enumerate a countable dense set of positive real points, starting with $1$. Apply <a href="#eq:complex-second-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:complex-second-moment">[eq:complex-second-moment]</a>–<a href="#eq:product-second-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:product-second-moment">[eq:product-second-moment]</a> successively on an exhaustion of $\mathbb H$, with errors tending to zero and with $T_n=t_nL_n^3\ge n$. At stage $n$ require the product estimates for the first $q$ points for each $q\le n$. This is a finite collection of requirements, so the same parameter choices can enforce all of them. The resulting random functions $F_n=\mathcal Z_{L_n,k_n,t_n}$ satisfy Lemma <a href="#lem:harmonic-selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:harmonic-selection">27</a>, by samplewise nonvanishing and the moment estimates. Its conclusion is precisely <a href="#eq:harmonic-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:harmonic-tangent">[eq:harmonic-tangent]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### All ergodic equilibrium states have the same radius

The negative square in <a href="#eq:negative-square-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-square-local">[eq:negative-square-local]</a> now separates two hypothetical magnetization radii: a state with smaller radius would dominate at small positive $x$, while the largest radius must dominate at large $x$. Harmonicity prevents this change.

<a id="stmt-prop-equilibrium-radius"></a>
<div id="prop:equilibrium-radius" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:equilibrium-radius"></a>
**Proposition 28** (Equilibrium magnetization radius). *Every ergodic translation-invariant state $\omega\in\mathcal E_\beta$ satisfies $$\omega(X_0)^2+\omega(Y_0)^2+\omega(Z_0)^2=m^2.
 \label{eq:equilibrium-radius}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-equilibrium-radius"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-equilibrium-radius</summary>

*Proof.* The maximum of $\omega(Y_0)$ over the compact face $\mathcal E_\beta$ is $m$. The maximizing states form a nonempty compact face, so it contains an extreme point $\omega_m$ of the translation-invariant state space. Thus $\omega_m$ is ergodic. The bound on the length of every equilibrium magnetization vector implies that its vector is $(0,m,0)$.

<a id="eq:two-radius-lower"></a>
Suppose an ergodic equilibrium state has magnetization length $m'<m$. Rotate it to a state $\omega_{m'}$ with vector $(0,m',0)$; rotations preserve equilibrium and translation ergodicity. By Lemma <a href="#lem:ergodic-variance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ergodic-variance">24</a>, for $r=m,m'$, $$\omega_r(Y_{C_B}^2/B^6)\longrightarrow r^2,
 \qquad
 \omega_r\bigl((X_{C_W}^2+Z_{C_W}^2)/W^6\bigr)
                                          \longrightarrow0.$$ For each $B$, choose $W(B)\ge2B$ so large that the second expectation, multiplied by $D_B$, is at most $1/B$ in both states. Then, writing $s_B=s_{B,W(B)}$, $$\liminf_{B\to\infty}s_B(x)
        \ge\max\{xm-m^2,\;xm'-(m')^2\},\qquad x>0.
 \label{eq:two-radius-lower}$$

<a id="eq:large-field-tangent"></a>
There is also an upper bound that is uniform in $B,W$. If $y=\omega(Y_0)$, positivity of variance and translation invariance give $$\omega(Y_{C_B}^2/B^6)\ge y^2,\qquad -m\le y\le m.$$ Dropping the other nonpositive square terms shows that $$s_{B,W}(x)\le\max_{-m\le y\le m}(xy-y^2)
                 =xm-m^2,\qquad x>2S,
 \label{eq:large-field-tangent}$$ because $m\le S$ and $y\mapsto xy-y^2$ is increasing on $[-m,m]$ for such $x$. For every $x>0$ we also have $s_{B,W}(x)\le Sx$.

Let $v_B$ be the harmonic function supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:harmonic-tangent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:harmonic-tangent">26</a>. It satisfies $v_B(z)\le S\Re z$ on $\mathbb H$. At any fixed $x_0>2S$, <a href="#eq:two-radius-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-radius-lower">[eq:two-radius-lower]</a> and <a href="#eq:large-field-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-field-tangent">[eq:large-field-tangent]</a> give $v_B(x_0)\to x_0m-m^2$. Harnack’s inequality applied to the nonnegative harmonic functions $S\Re z-v_B(z)$, followed by interior compactness, supplies a subsequential harmonic limit $v$ on $\mathbb H$. The same two inequalities imply $v(x)=xm-m^2$ for every $x>2S$. The restriction of a harmonic function to the real axis is real analytic, so this identity holds for every $x>0$.

On the other hand, <a href="#eq:two-radius-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-radius-lower">[eq:two-radius-lower]</a> passes to this subsequence and gives $v(x)\ge xm'-(m')^2$. For $0<x<m+m'$, these two assertions contradict $$\bigl(xm'-(m')^2\bigr)-\bigl(xm-m^2\bigr)
           =(m-m')(m+m'-x)>0.$$ Since $m>0$, this interval is nonempty even when $m'=0$. No smaller radius is possible, proving <a href="#eq:equilibrium-radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:equilibrium-radius">[eq:equilibrium-radius]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:global-spin"></a>
### The total-spin limit

We now combine spatial comparison with the identification of equilibrium magnetization lengths. Fix a finite $\beta$ above all the thresholds in the preceding sections and write $m=m_{S,\beta}>0$.

<a id="stmt-lem-local-second-moment"></a>
<div id="lem:local-second-moment" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:local-second-moment"></a>
**Lemma 29**. *For every finite nonempty rectangle $C\subset\mathbb Z^3$ and every translation-invariant equilibrium state $\omega$, $$\label{eq:local-second-moment}
 \sum_{a=1}^3\omega\!\left[
       \left(\frac1{|C|}\sum_{x\in C}S_x^a\right)^2\right]\ge m^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-second-moment"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-second-moment</summary>

*Proof.* In a translation-ergodic equilibrium state, translation invariance and positivity of variance give $$\omega\!\left[
       \left(\frac1{|C|}\sum_{x\in C}S_x^a\right)^2\right]
    \ge\omega(S_0^a)^2.$$ Summing and applying Proposition <a href="#prop:equilibrium-radius" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:equilibrium-radius">28</a> proves the assertion for extreme equilibrium states. The equilibrium set is a compact face of the translation-invariant state space, as recalled in Section <a href="#sec:equilibrium" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:equilibrium">6</a>. By the Krein–Milman theorem it is the closed convex hull of its extreme points. The left side of <a href="#eq:local-second-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-second-moment">[eq:local-second-moment]</a> is continuous and affine in the state, so the inequality holds throughout that face. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-global-second-moment"></a>
<div id="prop:global-second-moment" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:global-second-lower"></a>
**Proposition 30**. *The periodic zero-field Gibbs states satisfy $$\label{eq:global-second-lower}
 \liminf_{L\to\infty}\frac1{V^2}
        \sum_{a=1}^3\langle(M_L^a)^2\rangle\ge m^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-global-second-moment"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-global-second-moment</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a sequence of even side lengths realizing the lower limit in <a href="#eq:global-second-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:global-second-lower">[eq:global-second-lower]</a>, and pass to a subsequence on which the Gibbs states converge locally. Their limit $\omega$ is a translation-invariant equilibrium state by Section <a href="#sec:equilibrium" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:equilibrium">6</a>. Fix the terminal scale $R_*$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:scale-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:scale-comparison">22</a>. Its finest partition consists of rectangles with side lengths bounded above and below by constants depending on $R_*$ only. For this fixed $R_*$ there are finitely many possible rectangle shapes. Translation invariance and local convergence therefore imply, uniformly over all cells in that partition, $$\liminf_{L\to\infty}
 \frac1V\sum_{C\in\mathcal P_*}|C|
       \sum_{a=1}^3
       \left\langle
          \left(\frac1{|C|}\sum_{x\in C}S_x^a\right)^2
       \right\rangle
       \ge m^2,$$ where Lemma <a href="#lem:local-second-moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-second-moment">29</a> supplies the lower bound for each limiting shape. The spatial comparison <a href="#eq:spatial-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spatial-comparison">[eq:spatial-comparison]</a>, applied in each of the three directions, gives $$\liminf_{L\to\infty}\frac1{V^2}
       \sum_{a=1}^3\langle(M_L^a)^2\rangle
       \ge m^2-\frac{C_\beta}{\log R_*}.$$ Let $R_*\to\infty$. The sequence was chosen to realize the lower limit, so <a href="#eq:global-second-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:global-second-lower">[eq:global-second-lower]</a> follows along all even side lengths. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-global-second-moment-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-global-second-moment</summary>

<a id="eq:casimir"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* The Hamiltonian commutes with the global spin representation of $SU(2)$. Decompose the finite-volume Hilbert space into spin-$J$ irreducible representations and their multiplicity spaces. On each irreducible factor the Gibbs density is a scalar multiple of the identity. Thus the Gibbs trace defines a random label $J_L$ with $0\le J_L\le SV$, and $$\label{eq:casimir}
 \sum_{a=1}^3\langle(M_L^a)^2\rangle
       =\mathbb E[J_L(J_L+1)].$$ Conditional on $J_L=J$, a component in any fixed unit direction has the uniform distribution on $-J,-J+1,\ldots,J$.

We first verify the upper-tail claim <a href="#eq:overview-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:overview-upper">[eq:overview-upper]</a>. Fix $\epsilon>0$. By the definition of the right derivative, some fixed $h>0$ satisfies $$p_{S,\beta}(h)-p_{S,\beta}(0)
       \le h(m+\epsilon/4).$$ Since $H_{S,L}$ commutes with $M_L^3$, exponential Markov and the pressure limit give $$\begin{aligned}
 \limsup_{L\to\infty}\frac1V
   \log\mathbb P\bigl(M_L^3/V>m+\epsilon/2\bigr)
 &\le -\beta h(m+\epsilon/2)
       +\beta\bigl[p_{S,\beta}(h)-p_{S,\beta}(0)\bigr] \\
 &\le -\beta h\epsilon/4<0.
\end{aligned}$$ Here the probability is the spectral distribution of $M_L^3$ in the Gibbs state. If $J/V>m+\epsilon$, at least $\epsilon V/2-1$ of its $2J+1$ magnetic levels exceed $(m+\epsilon/2)V$. For all sufficiently large $L$, their conditional probability is bounded below by a positive constant depending only on $\epsilon,S$. Consequently $$\mathbb P(J_L/V>m+\epsilon)\longrightarrow0.$$

<a id="eq:spin-concentration"></a>
The laws of $J_L/V$ are supported in the compact interval $[0,S]$. Every subsequential limit is supported in $[0,m]$ by this upper-tail bound. By <a href="#eq:casimir" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:casimir">[eq:casimir]</a>, Proposition <a href="#prop:global-second-moment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:global-second-moment">30</a>, and $J_L/V^2\le S/V$, its second moment is at least $m^2$. A probability measure on $[0,m]$ with that property is the point mass at $m$. Hence $$\label{eq:spin-concentration}
 J_L/V\longrightarrow m\quad\text{in probability},
 \qquad
 \frac{\mathbb E[J_L(J_L+1)]}{V^2}\longrightarrow m^2.$$ The second conclusion proves <a href="#eq:second-moment-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:second-moment-law">[eq:second-moment-law]</a>.

For $t\ne0$, rotate the direction $t/|t|$ to the third axis. Conditional on $J_L=J$, the Laplace transform in <a href="#eq:spherical-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spherical-law">[eq:spherical-law]</a> is $$\frac1{2J+1}\sum_{r=-J}^{J}e^{|t|r/V}.$$ Whenever $J/V\to m>0$, these Riemann sums converge to $$\frac12\int_{-1}^1e^{m|t|u}\,\mathrm du
       =\frac{\sinh(m|t|)}{m|t|}.$$ They are uniformly bounded by $e^{S|t|}$, so <a href="#eq:spin-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spin-concentration">[eq:spin-concentration]</a> implies convergence of their expectations. The case $t=0$ is immediate. The normalized area measure on $\mathbb S^2$ has a uniform third coordinate in $[-1,1]$, which identifies the integral in <a href="#eq:spherical-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spherical-law">[eq:spherical-law]</a> and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "One real slot and its stationary ghost, shown on a cut-open time circle. An absent switch leaves the ghost as a separate pinned cycle. A present switch inserts its pinned line into the real cycle. In the unconditioned fresh real-hole traversal killed on entry into a ghost, passage through this layer survives with probability $w = 1 − q$. The rest of the real exchange picture is suppressed.",
      "figure_id": "fig:sparse-ghost",
      "pdf_page": 12,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:sparse-ghost",
          "exact_quote": "One real slot and its stationary ghost, shown on a cut-open\n time circle.  An absent switch leaves the ghost as a separate pinned\n cycle.  A present switch inserts its pinned line into the real cycle.\n In the unconditioned fresh real-hole t",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth,font=\\small]\n \\foreach \\x/\\lab in {0/{no switch},5.3/{switch}} {\n  \\node at (\\x+1.3,3.25) {\\lab};\n  \\draw[->,thick] (\\x+.4,.4)--(\\x+.4,2.5);\n  \\draw[->,thick,dashed] (\\x+2.2,.4)--(\\x+2.2,2.5);\n  \\node[above] at (\\x+.4,2.5) {real};\n  \\node[above] at (\\x+2.2,2.5) {ghost};\n  \\fill (\\x+2.2,1.45) circle (2.5pt);\n  \\node[right] at (\\x+2.2,1.45) {pin};\n  \\node[left] at (\\x+.35,.4) {\\(0^+\\)};\n  \\node[left] at (\\x+.35,2.3) {\\(\\beta^-\\)};\n }\n \\draw[thick] (.4,.4)--(.4,.05);\n \\draw[thick,dashed] (2.2,.4)--(2.2,.05);\n \\node at (1.3,-.3) {base weight \\(w\\)};\n \\draw[thick] (5.7,.05)--(7.5,.4);\n \\draw[thick,dashed] (7.5,.05)--(5.7,.4);\n \\node at (6.6,-.3) {base weight \\(q\\)};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two side-by-side vertical line diagrams labeled \"no switch\" (left) and \"switch\" (right). Both depict two parallel vertical tracks: a solid vertical line on the left labeled \"real\" with an upward arrow pointing to $\\beta^-$ and starting at $0^+$, and a dashed vertical line on the right labeled \"ghost\" containing a solid black circle marked \"pin\". Under \"no switch\", the two lines remain separate, labeled below as \"base weight $w$\". Under \"switch\", a dashed connection links the real and ghost tracks near the bottom, labeled below as \"base weight $q$\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "Two parts of the hole comparison, drawn schematically. Left: a fresh walk jumps between skeleton lines during a contact interval. Comparing both cut values through $u_{ϕ(j)}$ charges energy only to the cut starts $i, j$. Right: a path in $Γ$ first exits a filled coarse hull. Clearance makes its crossing box good; the absence of long mark paths then confines the whole exiting link near that box. A surviving exterior-boundary route leads to $i(r_{H})$, which defines $U_{B}$ on the hull. Gray squares indicate bad boxes; the larger gray region represents the filled hull, with distances and thicknesses not to scale.",

      "pdf_page": 28,

          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],

\\node[font=\\small\\bfseries] at (2.4,4.25) {A contact edge};\n    \\draw[->,gray] (0,3.65)--(5.25,3.65)\n      node[pos=.5,above] {one period};\n    \\draw[gray] (0,3.5)--(0,.3);\n    \\draw[gray] (5,3.5)--(5,.3);\n    \\node[above] at (0,3.65) {$0$};\n    \\node[above] at (5,3.65) {$\\beta$};\n    \\fill[gray!15] (2.1,.4) rectangle (2.85,3.2);\n    \\draw[thick] (0,2.8)--(1.1,2.8)--(1.1,2.35)\n      --(3.3,2.35)--(3.3,2.9)--(5,2.9);\n    \\draw[thick] (0,.9)--(.8,.9)--(.8,1.35)\n      --(3.9,1.35)--(3.9,.9)--(5,.9);\n    \\node[hole,label=left:$i$] at (0,2.8) {};\n    \\node[hole,label=left:$j$] at (0,.9) {};\n    \\node[hole,label=right:$\\phi(i)$] at (5,2.9) {};\n    \\node[hole,label=right:$\\phi(j)$] at (5,.9) {};\n    \\draw[very thick,->] (2.5,2.35)--(2.5,1.35);\n    \\node[right] at (2.58,1.84) {jump};\n    \\draw[<->] (2.1,.27)--(2.85,.27)\n       node[midway,below] {$\\tau\\ge\\delta$};\n    \\node[align=center] at (2.6,-.65)\n a filled hull};\n    \\draw[step=.5,gray!18] (-.25,.2) grid (4.75,3.7);\n    \\filldraw[fill=gray!12,draw=gray!60,thick]\n       (.3,.65)--(3.2,.65)--(3.2,2.7)--(2.45,2.7)\n       --(2.45,3.15)--(.3,3.15)--cycle;\n    \\node at (1,2.7) {$H$};\n    \\fill[gray!60] (1.2,1.8) rectangle (1.7,2.3);\n    \\fill[gray!60] (1.7,1.3) rectangle (2.2,1.8);\n    \\node[font=\\scriptsize,align=center] at (1.55,1.03)\n      {bad boxes};\n    \\node[hole,label=left:$j$] (j) at (.7,1.8) {};\n    \\node[hole] (v1) at (1.2,1.45) {};\n    \\node[hole] (v2) at (2.5,1.5) {};\n    \\node[hole] (v3) at (3.45,1.85) {};\n    \\draw[thick,->] (j)--(v1);\n    \\draw[thick,->] (v1)--(v2);\n    \\draw[very thick,->] (v2)--(v3);\n    \\draw[rounded corners,thick,->]\n      (v3)--(3.65,1.85)--(3.65,3.45)--(2.9,3.45);\n    \\node[hole,label=above:$i(r_H)$] at (2.9,3.45) {};\n    \\draw[gray,dashed] (3.08,1.4) rectangle (3.92,2.3);\n    \\node[font=\\scriptsize,align=left,anchor=west]\n      at (3.96,1.85) {good\\\\boundary};\n    \\node[align=center] at (2.25,-.65)\n      {first exit, then an exterior route\\\\\n       to the comparison value};\n  \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two schematic diagrams.\nLeft diagram (\"A contact edge\"): Shows two horizontal lines from time 0 to $\\beta$ (\"one period\"), the upper line labeled $i$ on the left and pointing with an arrow to $\\phi(i)$ on the right, the lower line labeled $j$ on the left and pointing with an arrow to $\\phi(j)$ on the right. A downward vertical arrow labeled \"jump\" connects line $i$ to line $j$ within an interval of length $\\tau \\ge \\delta$. Below the diagram are the labels \"$i \\longrightarrow \\phi(j) \\longleftarrow j$\" and \"cost uses $e_i + e_j$\".\nRight diagram (\"Leaving a filled hull\"): Shows a shaded polygon representing the filled coarse hull $H$ containing multiple small gray squares labeled \"bad boxes\". A trajectory starting from $j$ inside $H$ exits across a dashed contour labeled \"good boundary\", passing through a box and following an exterior path outside $H$ to a node labeled $i(r_H)$. Text beneath reads \"first exit, then an exterior route / to the comparison value\"."
